While I can picture the truck spinning when I think back on it, I'm not having those vivid, unwanted memories popping up out of nowhere while I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, or have you noticed any other physical symptoms like seizures or unusual headaches since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, and I haven't experienced any seizures or unusual headaches since the accident. As for the difficulty thinking, it has been a constant, persistent struggle ever since the accident, especially whenever I sit down to work or try to make simple decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels unreal or dreamlike since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where it happened. It feels like I'm not really inside my own body, but just floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you ever felt like you have different parts of your personality that take control at different times, or have you discovered items or notes you don't remember acquiring?"

No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I've never felt like different parts of my personality were taking over, and I haven't found any items or notes that I don't remember getting.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife pushed me to get help because it is severely affecting my ability to manage my tax business and handle things at home.\n\nDoctor: Did you lose consciousness, experience a head injury, or have any memory gaps immediately following the car accident?\n\nPatient: The ER doctors said I didn't have any head injuries, just some minor bumps and scrapes, but I definitely have a major memory gap. I remember seeing the headlights coming at me and the truck spinning, but after that, there's just a big empty hole until I was suddenly outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I can't fill in those minutes in between.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted, vivid memories of the crash or feeling like you are reliving the accident when you are awake?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of flashbacks or feel like I'm reliving the crash during the day. While I can picture the truck spinning when I think back on it, I'm not having those vivid, unwanted memories popping up out of nowhere while I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, or have you noticed any other physical symptoms like seizures or unusual headaches since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, and I haven't experienced any seizures or unusual headaches since the accident. The doctors at the ER checked me over and said I only had minor physical injuries, so physically I've been okay.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed your memory gaps or difficulty thinking clearly getting worse when you are under stress or discussing the crash, or is this a constant, persistent state?\n\nPatient: The memory gap is a constant, specific blank spot—that chunk of time between seeing the headlights and waking up on the stretcher is just completely gone all the time. As for the difficulty thinking, it has been a constant, persistent struggle ever since the accident, especially whenever I sit down to work or try to make simple decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels unreal or dreamlike since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where it happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, almost like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not really inside my own body, but just floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you ever felt like you have different parts of your personality that take control at different times, or have you discovered items or notes you don't remember acquiring?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I've never felt like different parts of my personality were taking over, and I haven't found any items or notes that I don't remember getting.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being detached from your body and the difficulty with your thinking been present consistently every day since the accident, or do they come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: The difficulty with my thinking has been constant every single day since the accident, especially when I try to work. On the other hand, the feeling of being detached from my body comes in waves, mostly when I'm riding in a car or near that intersection. Both of these issues started right after the wreck two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing significant sadness, anxiety, or a sense of being overwhelmed that feels like a struggle to adjust to the life changes caused by the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel incredibly anxious and overwhelmed, and I'm constantly on edge scanning for danger. Even simple daily decisions like picking which email to answer first feel impossible, making it very hard to adjust to these changes. It has gotten to the point where my wife has had to take over my responsibilities, like paying the bills, because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current difficulty with thinking and detachment is primarily a reaction to the stress of the accident and the life changes it caused, or does it feel like a separate, involuntary 'shutting down' of your identity or memory that is independent of your emotional distress?"

The memory gap, my mind blanking out at work, and those weird out-of-body feelings make me feel like my brain is stalling or disconnecting on its own, completely out of my control.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you experienced any periods where you felt like you had lost your sense of identity or felt like a completely different person, or are these dissociative feelings strictly limited to the memory gap and the sensation of being detached from your physical body?"

house, and even sitting in my own living room I find myself scanning for exits and listening for sirens like something bad is about to happen again. \n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now if my wife comes into my office without me hearing her footsteps first, or a car door slams outside, I jerk so hard I almost come out of my chair, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes a long time to calm myself back down. \n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially when I’m riding in a car or walking past the intersection where it happened, when everything feels weirdly far away and fake, like I’m watching a movie of my own life and I’m not really inside my body, just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last two weeks I’ve fallen so far behind that I’ve had to ask clients for extensions, missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over simple things like paying bills and organizing appointments because I can’t keep up or think clearly enough to manage my work and home responsibilities.\n- duration: All of this started right after the car wreck two weeks ago and has been going on constantly since then.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife pushed me to get help because it is severely affecting my ability to manage my tax business and handle things at home.\n\nDoctor: Did you lose consciousness, experience a head injury, or have any memory gaps immediately following the car accident?\n\nPatient: The ER doctors said I didn't have any head injuries, just some minor bumps and scrapes, but I definitely have a major memory gap. I remember seeing the headlights coming at me and the truck spinning, but after that, there's just a big empty hole until I was suddenly outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I can't fill in those minutes in between.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted, vivid memories of the crash or feeling like you are reliving the accident when you are awake?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of flashbacks or feel like I'm reliving the crash during the day. While I can picture the truck spinning when I think back on it, I'm not having those vivid, unwanted memories popping up out of nowhere while I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, or have you noticed any other physical symptoms like seizures or unusual headaches since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, and I haven't experienced any seizures or unusual headaches since the accident. The doctors at the ER checked me over and said I only had minor physical injuries, so physically I've been okay.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed your memory gaps or difficulty thinking clearly getting worse when you are under stress or discussing the crash, or is this a constant, persistent state?\n\nPatient: The memory gap is a constant, specific blank spot—that chunk of time between seeing the headlights and waking up on the stretcher is just completely gone all the time. As for the difficulty thinking, it has been a constant, persistent struggle ever since the accident, especially whenever I sit down to work or try to make simple decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels unreal or dreamlike since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where it happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, almost like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not really inside my own body, but just floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you ever felt like you have different parts of your personality that take control at different times, or have you discovered items or notes you don't remember acquiring?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I've never felt like different parts of my personality were taking over, and I haven't found any items or notes that I don't remember getting.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being detached from your body and the difficulty with your thinking been present consistently every day since the accident, or do they come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: The difficulty with my thinking has been constant every single day since the accident, especially when I try to work. On the other hand, the feeling of being detached from my body comes in waves, mostly when I'm riding in a car or near that intersection. Both of these issues started right after the wreck two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing significant sadness, anxiety, or a sense of being overwhelmed that feels like a struggle to adjust to the life changes caused by the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel incredibly anxious and overwhelmed, and I'm constantly on edge scanning for danger. Even simple daily decisions like picking which email to answer first feel impossible, making it very hard to adjust to these changes. It has gotten to the point where my wife has had to take over my responsibilities, like paying the bills, because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current difficulty with thinking and detachment is primarily a reaction to the stress of the accident and the life changes it caused, or does it feel like a separate, involuntary 'shutting down' of your identity or memory that is independent of your emotional distress?\n\nPatient: While it all absolutely started with the wreck, this feels like an involuntary, automatic shutting down rather than just normal stress. The memory gap, my mind blanking out at work, and those weird out-of-body feelings make me feel like my brain is stalling or disconnecting on its own, completely out of my control.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you experienced any periods where you felt like you had lost your sense of identity or felt like a completely different person, or are these dissociative feelings strictly limited to the memory gap and the sensation of being detached from your physical body?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't lost my sense of identity or felt like a different person at all. These feelings are strictly limited to that blank spot in my memory from the crash and those moments where I feel detached from my physical body, like I'm watching myself from behind. I still know exactly who I am, it's just that my brain and body feel disconnected in those moments.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of detachment, does the world around you feel completely unreal or dreamlike, and do you find it difficult to recall personal information other than the specific memories of the accident?"

But I don't have any trouble remembering my general personal information; it is really just that one frustrating blank spot in my memory covering those few minutes of the crash.

His wife, alarmed by how unlike himself he seemed and how his business was suddenly at risk, pushed him to see his primary care doctor, who referred him for an urgent psychiatric evaluation when it became clear his post‑accident mental state was significantly disrupting his ability to function as a self‑employed consultant.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): When I sit down at my desk to work on a return, I read the same line of numbers over and over and my mind just blanks out, like the gears won’t catch, and even picking which email to answer first feels overwhelming and I just stare at the screen. \n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I can picture the truck spinning and the sound of metal crunching clear as day, but there’s this big empty hole in my memory between seeing the headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher, and no matter how hard I try I can’t fill in what actually happened in those minutes. \n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): My wife tells me good things, like a client saying they’re glad I’m okay or my son sending me a funny video, and I know I should feel happy or grateful, but it all just kind of bounces off me and I feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life. \n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Ever since the crash I’m constantly on edge, checking the windows, jumping up at every car that drives by the house, and even sitting in my own living room I find myself scanning for exits and listening for sirens like something bad is about to happen again. \n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now if my wife comes into my office without me hearing her footsteps first, or a car door slams outside, I jerk so hard I almost come out of my chair, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes a long time to calm myself back down. \n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially when I’m riding in a car or walking past the intersection where it happened, when everything feels weirdly far away and fake, like I’m watching a movie of my own life and I’m not really inside my body, just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last two weeks I’ve fallen so far behind that I’ve had to ask clients for extensions, missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over simple things like paying bills and organizing appointments because I can’t keep up or think clearly enough to manage my work and home responsibilities.\n- duration: All of this started right after the car wreck two weeks ago and has been going on constantly since then.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization)\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that you do experience those 'out-of-body' and dreamlike sensations, but clarify that they are actually tied to reminders of the accident—specifically when you are riding in a car or walking past the intersection where it happened. Describe the feeling of watching your life like a movie and floating behind yourself, emphasizing that it doesn't seem to happen randomly in completely unrelated situations."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife pushed me to get help because it is severely affecting my ability to manage my tax business and handle things at home.\n\nDoctor: Did you lose consciousness, experience a head injury, or have any memory gaps immediately following the car accident?\n\nPatient: The ER doctors said I didn't have any head injuries, just some minor bumps and scrapes, but I definitely have a major memory gap. I remember seeing the headlights coming at me and the truck spinning, but after that, there's just a big empty hole until I was suddenly outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I can't fill in those minutes in between.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted, vivid memories of the crash or feeling like you are reliving the accident when you are awake?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of flashbacks or feel like I'm reliving the crash during the day. While I can picture the truck spinning when I think back on it, I'm not having those vivid, unwanted memories popping up out of nowhere while I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, or have you noticed any other physical symptoms like seizures or unusual headaches since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, and I haven't experienced any seizures or unusual headaches since the accident. The doctors at the ER checked me over and said I only had minor physical injuries, so physically I've been okay.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed your memory gaps or difficulty thinking clearly getting worse when you are under stress or discussing the crash, or is this a constant, persistent state?\n\nPatient: The memory gap is a constant, specific blank spot—that chunk of time between seeing the headlights and waking up on the stretcher is just completely gone all the time. As for the difficulty thinking, it has been a constant, persistent struggle ever since the accident, especially whenever I sit down to work or try to make simple decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels unreal or dreamlike since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where it happened. It feels like I'm not really inside my own body, but just floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you ever felt like you have different parts of your personality that take control at different times, or have you discovered items or notes you don't remember acquiring?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I've never felt like different parts of my personality were taking over, and I haven't found any items or notes that I don't remember getting.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being detached from your body and the difficulty with your thinking been present consistently every day since the accident, or do they come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: The difficulty with my thinking has been constant every single day since the accident, especially when I try to work. It has gotten to the point where my wife has had to take over my responsibilities, like paying the bills, because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current difficulty with thinking and detachment is primarily a reaction to the stress of the accident and the life changes it caused, or does it feel like a separate, involuntary 'shutting down' of your identity or memory that is independent of your emotional distress?\n\nPatient: While it all absolutely started with the wreck, this feels like an involuntary, automatic shutting down rather than just normal stress. The memory gap, my mind blanking out at work, and those weird out-of-body feelings make me feel like my brain is stalling or disconnecting on its own, completely out of my control.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you experienced any periods where you felt like you had lost your sense of identity or felt like a completely different person, or are these dissociative feelings strictly limited to the memory gap and the sensation of being detached from your physical body?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't lost my sense of identity or felt like a different person at all. These feelings are strictly limited to that blank spot in my memory from the crash and those moments where I feel detached from my physical body, like I'm watching myself from behind. I still know exactly who I am, it's just that my brain and body feel disconnected in those moments.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of detachment, does the world around you feel completely unreal or dreamlike, and do you find it difficult to recall personal information other than the specific memories of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those moments of detachment—especially when I'm in a car or near that intersection—everything around me feels fake, far away, and like I'm watching a movie. But I don't have any trouble remembering my general personal information; it is really just that one frustrating blank spot in my memory covering those few minutes of the crash.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the specific memory gap of the crash, do you find that you frequently experience these 'dreamlike' or 'out-of-body' sensations in situations unrelated to the accident or reminders of it?"

He was trapped for several minutes while first responders worked to get him out, watching the other driver unconscious and hearing conflicting comments about a possible fuel leak and fire risk, which left him convinced he might die there on the road. Although he was discharged from the ER the same night with only minor physical injuries, he tried to return to his home‑based tax work and weekend fishing routine within days, only to find that he could not reliably get through basic client files or even organize his schedule, leading to missed calls, angry emails, and a near‑lost long‑term client. His wife, alarmed by how unlike himself he seemed and how his business was suddenly at risk, pushed him to see his primary care doctor, who referred him for an urgent psychiatric evaluation when it became clear his post‑accident mental state was significantly disrupting his ability to function as a self‑employed consultant.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): When I sit down at my desk to work on a return, I read the same line of numbers over and over and my mind just blanks out, like the gears won’t catch, and even picking which email to answer first feels overwhelming and I just stare at the screen. \n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I can picture the truck spinning and the sound of metal crunching clear as day, but there’s this big empty hole in my memory between seeing the headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher, and no matter how hard I try I can’t fill in what actually happened in those minutes. \n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): My wife tells me good things, like a client saying they’re glad I’m okay or my son sending me a funny video, and I know I should feel happy or grateful, but it all just kind of bounces off me and I feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life. \n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Ever since the crash I’m constantly on edge, checking the windows, jumping up at every car that drives by the house, and even sitting in my own living room I find myself scanning for exits and listening for sirens like something bad is about to happen again. \n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now if my wife comes into my office without me hearing her footsteps first, or a car door slams outside, I jerk so hard I almost come out of my chair, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes a long time to calm myself back down. \n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially when I’m riding in a car or walking past the intersection where it happened, when everything feels weirdly far away and fake, like I’m watching a movie of my own life and I’m not really inside my body, just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last two weeks I’ve fallen so far behind that I’ve had to ask clients for extensions, missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over simple things like paying bills and organizing appointments because I can’t keep up or think clearly enough to manage my work and home responsibilities.\n- duration: All of this started right after the car wreck two weeks ago and has been going on constantly since then.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S079 (Hypervigilance), S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response)\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that you are constantly on edge and easily startled, even when you are just sitting at home. Describe how you are always scanning for exits and checking the windows in your own living room, and how you jump out of your chair with a racing heart if your wife walks into your office without you hearing her first or if a car door slams outside."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife pushed me to get help because it is severely affecting my ability to manage my tax business and handle things at home.\n\nDoctor: Did you lose consciousness, experience a head injury, or have any memory gaps immediately following the car accident?\n\nPatient: The ER doctors said I didn't have any head injuries, just some minor bumps and scrapes, but I definitely have a major memory gap. I remember seeing the headlights coming at me and the truck spinning, but after that, there's just a big empty hole until I was suddenly outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I can't fill in those minutes in between.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted, vivid memories of the crash or feeling like you are reliving the accident when you are awake?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of flashbacks or feel like I'm reliving the crash during the day. While I can picture the truck spinning when I think back on it, I'm not having those vivid, unwanted memories popping up out of nowhere while I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, or have you noticed any other physical symptoms like seizures or unusual headaches since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, and I haven't experienced any seizures or unusual headaches since the accident. The doctors at the ER checked me over and said I only had minor physical injuries, so physically I've been okay.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed your memory gaps or difficulty thinking clearly getting worse when you are under stress or discussing the crash, or is this a constant, persistent state?\n\nPatient: The memory gap is a constant, specific blank spot—that chunk of time between seeing the headlights and waking up on the stretcher is just completely gone all the time. As for the difficulty thinking, it has been a constant, persistent struggle ever since the accident, especially whenever I sit down to work or try to make simple decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels unreal or dreamlike since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where it happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, almost like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not really inside my own body, but just floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you ever felt like you have different parts of your personality that take control at different times, or have you discovered items or notes you don't remember acquiring?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I've never felt like different parts of my personality were taking over, and I haven't found any items or notes that I don't remember getting.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being detached from your body and the difficulty with your thinking been present consistently every day since the accident, or do they come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: The difficulty with my thinking has been constant every single day since the accident, especially when I try to work. On the other hand, the feeling of being detached from my body comes in waves, mostly when I'm riding in a car or near that intersection. Both of these issues started right after the wreck two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing significant sadness, anxiety, or a sense of being overwhelmed that feels like a struggle to adjust to the life changes caused by the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel incredibly anxious and overwhelmed, and I'm constantly on edge scanning for danger. Even simple daily decisions like picking which email to answer first feel impossible, making it very hard to adjust to these changes. It has gotten to the point where my wife has had to take over my responsibilities, like paying the bills, because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current difficulty with thinking and detachment is primarily a reaction to the stress of the accident and the life changes it caused, or does it feel like a separate, involuntary 'shutting down' of your identity or memory that is independent of your emotional distress?\n\nPatient: While it all absolutely started with the wreck, this feels like an involuntary, automatic shutting down rather than just normal stress. The memory gap, my mind blanking out at work, and those weird out-of-body feelings make me feel like my brain is stalling or disconnecting on its own, completely out of my control.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you experienced any periods where you felt like you had lost your sense of identity or felt like a completely different person, or are these dissociative feelings strictly limited to the memory gap and the sensation of being detached from your physical body?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't lost my sense of identity or felt like a different person at all. These feelings are strictly limited to that blank spot in my memory from the crash and those moments where I feel detached from my physical body, like I'm watching myself from behind. I still know exactly who I am, it's just that my brain and body feel disconnected in those moments.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of detachment, does the world around you feel completely unreal or dreamlike, and do you find it difficult to recall personal information other than the specific memories of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those moments of detachment—especially when I'm in a car or near that intersection—everything around me feels fake, far away, and like I'm watching a movie. But I don't have any trouble remembering my general personal information; it is really just that one frustrating blank spot in my memory covering those few minutes of the crash.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the specific memory gap of the crash, do you find that you frequently experience these 'dreamlike' or 'out-of-body' sensations in situations unrelated to the accident or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: No, they don't happen randomly in completely unrelated situations. Those dreamlike, out-of-body feelings are strictly tied to reminders of the accident, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past that intersection. In those moments, I feel like I'm watching my life like a movie and floating behind myself, but it doesn't just pop up out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing persistent symptoms like difficulty sleeping, being easily startled, or feeling constantly on edge, even when you are not near the intersection or reminded of the crash?"

It takes a really long time to calm back down.

He was trapped for several minutes while first responders worked to get him out, watching the other driver unconscious and hearing conflicting comments about a possible fuel leak and fire risk, which left him convinced he might die there on the road. Although he was discharged from the ER the same night with only minor physical injuries, he tried to return to his home‑based tax work and weekend fishing routine within days, only to find that he could not reliably get through basic client files or even organize his schedule, leading to missed calls, angry emails, and a near‑lost long‑term client. His wife, alarmed by how unlike himself he seemed and how his business was suddenly at risk, pushed him to see his primary care doctor, who referred him for an urgent psychiatric evaluation when it became clear his post‑accident mental state was significantly disrupting his ability to function as a self‑employed consultant.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight on a return, I read the same line of numbers over and over and my mind just blanks out, like the gears won’t catch, and even picking which email to answer first feels overwhelming and I just stare at the screen. \n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I can picture the truck spinning and the sound of metal crunching clear as day, but there’s this big empty hole in my memory between seeing the headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher, and no matter how hard I try I can’t fill in what actually happened in those minutes. \n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): My wife tells me good things, like a client saying they’re glad I’m okay or my son sending me a funny video, and I know I should feel happy or grateful, but it all just kind of bounces off me and I feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life. \n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Ever since the crash I’m constantly on edge, checking the windows, jumping up at every car that drives by the house, and even sitting in my own living room I find myself scanning for exits and listening for sirens like something bad is about to happen again. \n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now if my wife comes into my office without me hearing her footsteps first, or a car door slams outside, I jerk so hard I almost come out of my chair, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes a long time to calm myself back down. \n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially when I’m riding in a car or walking past the intersection where it happened, when everything feels weirdly far away and fake, like I’m watching a movie of my own life and I’m not really inside my body, just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last two weeks I’ve fallen so far behind that I’ve had to ask clients for extensions, missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over simple things like paying bills and organizing appointments because I can’t keep up or think clearly enough to manage my work and home responsibilities.\n- duration: All of this started right after the car wreck two weeks ago and has been going on constantly since then.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife pushed me to get help because it is severely affecting my ability to manage my tax business and handle things at home.\n\nDoctor: Did you lose consciousness, experience a head injury, or have any memory gaps immediately following the car accident?\n\nPatient: The ER doctors said I didn't have any head injuries, just some minor bumps and scrapes, but I definitely have a major memory gap. I remember seeing the headlights coming at me and the truck spinning, but after that, there's just a big empty hole until I was suddenly outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I can't fill in those minutes in between.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted, vivid memories of the crash or feeling like you are reliving the accident when you are awake?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of flashbacks or feel like I'm reliving the crash during the day. While I can picture the truck spinning when I think back on it, I'm not having those vivid, unwanted memories popping up out of nowhere while I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, or have you noticed any other physical symptoms like seizures or unusual headaches since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been diagnosed with any neurological conditions, and I haven't experienced any seizures or unusual headaches since the accident. The doctors at the ER checked me over and said I only had minor physical injuries, so physically I've been okay.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed your memory gaps or difficulty thinking clearly getting worse when you are under stress or discussing the crash, or is this a constant, persistent state?\n\nPatient: The memory gap is a constant, specific blank spot—that chunk of time between seeing the headlights and waking up on the stretcher is just completely gone all the time. As for the difficulty thinking, it has been a constant, persistent struggle ever since the accident, especially whenever I sit down to work or try to make simple decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels unreal or dreamlike since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where it happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, almost like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not really inside my own body, but just floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you ever felt like you have different parts of your personality that take control at different times, or have you discovered items or notes you don't remember acquiring?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I've never felt like different parts of my personality were taking over, and I haven't found any items or notes that I don't remember getting.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being detached from your body and the difficulty with your thinking been present consistently every day since the accident, or do they come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: The difficulty with my thinking has been constant every single day since the accident, especially when I try to work. On the other hand, the feeling of being detached from my body comes in waves, mostly when I'm riding in a car or near that intersection. Both of these issues started right after the wreck two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing significant sadness, anxiety, or a sense of being overwhelmed that feels like a struggle to adjust to the life changes caused by the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel incredibly anxious and overwhelmed, and I'm constantly on edge scanning for danger. Even simple daily decisions like picking which email to answer first feel impossible, making it very hard to adjust to these changes. It has gotten to the point where my wife has had to take over my responsibilities, like paying the bills, because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current difficulty with thinking and detachment is primarily a reaction to the stress of the accident and the life changes it caused, or does it feel like a separate, involuntary 'shutting down' of your identity or memory that is independent of your emotional distress?\n\nPatient: While it all absolutely started with the wreck, this feels like an involuntary, automatic shutting down rather than just normal stress. The memory gap, my mind blanking out at work, and those weird out-of-body feelings make me feel like my brain is stalling or disconnecting on its own, completely out of my control.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you experienced any periods where you felt like you had lost your sense of identity or felt like a completely different person, or are these dissociative feelings strictly limited to the memory gap and the sensation of being detached from your physical body?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't lost my sense of identity or felt like a different person at all. These feelings are strictly limited to that blank spot in my memory from the crash and those moments where I feel detached from my physical body, like I'm watching myself from behind. I still know exactly who I am, it's just that my brain and body feel disconnected in those moments.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of detachment, does the world around you feel completely unreal or dreamlike, and do you find it difficult to recall personal information other than the specific memories of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those moments of detachment—especially when I'm in a car or near that intersection—everything around me feels fake, far away, and like I'm watching a movie. But I don't have any trouble remembering my general personal information; it is really just that one frustrating blank spot in my memory covering those few minutes of the crash.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the specific memory gap of the crash, do you find that you frequently experience these 'dreamlike' or 'out-of-body' sensations in situations unrelated to the accident or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: No, they don't happen randomly in completely unrelated situations. Those dreamlike, out-of-body feelings are strictly tied to reminders of the accident, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past that intersection. In those moments, I feel like I'm watching my life like a movie and floating behind myself, but it doesn't just pop up out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing persistent symptoms like difficulty sleeping, being easily startled, or feeling constantly on edge, even when you are not near the intersection or reminded of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am constantly on edge and easily startled, even just sitting at home where I find myself checking the windows and scanning for exits. If a car door slams outside or my wife walks into my office without me hearing her first, I jerk so hard I almost come out of my chair with my heart racing. It takes a really long time to calm back down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your primary distress is centered around these dissociative 'shutting down' experiences, or is it the persistent anxiety, startle response, and inability to function that bothers you the most?"

In those moments, I feel like I'm watching my life like a movie and floating behind myself, but it doesn't just pop up out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing persistent symptoms like difficulty sleeping, being easily startled, or feeling constantly on edge, even when you are not near the intersection or reminded of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am constantly on edge and easily startled, even just sitting at home where I find myself checking the windows and scanning for exits. If a car door slams outside or my wife walks into my office without me hearing her first, I jerk so hard I almost come out of my chair with my heart racing. It takes a really long time to calm back down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your primary distress is centered around these dissociative 'shutting down' experiences, or is it the persistent anxiety, startle response, and inability to function that bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: While both are highly distressing, the inability to think clearly and do my work is definitely the most stressful part because it is threatening my livelihood and forcing my wife to take over my responsibilities. The constant jumpiness and those weird out-of-body feelings are terrifying, but the practical impact of not being able to manage my daily life is what is causing the most distress."
  }
]

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
